The Gravity Restaurant SOP Library is part of The Gravity Standards Library within The Gravity System. Thomas
Monroe Books is the publisher.
The original purchaser may reproduce, adapt, post, and use the SOP pages in this book for internal training and
operational use at one restaurant location. This limited license does not permit resale, public distribution,
commercial duplication, posting the complete book online, or sharing the files with unrelated businesses.
These procedures are general operational templates. Each restaurant must customize them to its concept,
equipment, menu, staffing model, insurance requirements, company policies, and all applicable federal, state, and
local laws, health codes, fire codes, accessibility requirements, alcohol-service rules, employment rules, and
regulatory guidance.
Nothing in this book replaces legal, accounting, human-resources, food-safety, medical, emergency-response, or
regulatory advice. When a law, official code, manufacturer instruction, or approved company policy conflicts with a
template in this book, the controlling requirement must be followed.


HOW TO USE THIS LIBRARY
• Choose the SOP that most closely matches the process you need. Do not adopt it unchanged simply because it
appears in this book.
• Customize the responsibility, tools, timing, steps, performance standards, escalation authority, local requirements,
and approved forms.
• Assign an SOP identification number and owner. Record the version, effective date, approval, and next review
date before training employees.
• Train the employee using explanation, demonstration, supervised practice, real-shift observation, and manager
verification.
• Post only the approved version. Remove or clearly retire obsolete versions so employees are not trained from
conflicting standards.
• Review high-risk and high-frequency SOPs more often than low-risk procedures. Document changes and retrain
affected employees.

THE TWO-PAGE SOP STANDARD
Page 1 is the controlled procedure: purpose, scope, responsibility, tools, critical standards, and numbered steps.
Page 2 is the management page: verification checklist, common mistakes, customization fields, training sign-off,
and revision reminder.
Keep both pages of each SOP together. A procedure is not approved for use until the restaurant completes the
customization fields, assigns ownership, trains affected employees, and records manager approval.
Use Restaurant SOP & Station Operations Builder when the restaurant needs SOP-building instruction, station
posters, rollout tools, governance, readiness verification, or document-control training. This library supplies ready-to-
customize procedures rather than replacing that builder system.

OPERATIONAL GLOSSARY


PART 1

HOST, RESERVATIONS & GUEST
ARRIVAL

20 ready-to-customize SOPs
These SOPs control the guest's first and last impression, table flow, reservations, wait communication,
accessibility, large-party planning, and host-position continuity.


1. Host Stand Opening SOP

SOP ID V1-001 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To prepare the host stand, entrance, reservation tools, seating plan, and guest-arrival area before the
restaurant opens.
APPLIES TO: Hosts, lead hosts, opening managers, and any employee assigned to guest-arrival readiness.
RESPONSIBILITY: The opening host completes the setup. The manager on duty verifies readiness before the first
guest is seated.
WHEN USED: Before every opening period and again before any major shift change or high-volume reservation
block.
• Current floor plan and server section map
• Reservation and waitlist system
• Menus, children's menus, accessibility information, and promotional materials
• Charged phone, radio, tablet, pager, or other communication device
• Clean host stand supplies and manager notes
• The entrance, lobby, host stand, and guest path are clean, safe, and unobstructed.
• Reservations, large parties, closed tables, accessibility needs, and server sections are reviewed before opening.
• Only current menus, policies, and approved guest information are displayed.
• The host knows which manager is responsible for seating decisions and escalations.
1. Clock in and review the manager communication log, reservation notes, events, promotions, and expected business level.
2. Inspect the entrance, lobby, door glass, floor, mats, signage, lighting, and guest path; correct or report problems.
3. Clean and organize the host stand. Remove outdated papers, menus, signs, and personal items.
4. Power on and test the phone, reservation system, waitlist system, pagers, tablets, radios, and charging equipment.
5. Count and organize menus, children's menus, drink menus, and other approved materials.
6. Review the floor plan, server sections, closed sections, reserved tables, large-party setups, and accessibility seating options.
7. Confirm which tables are clean, stable, correctly set, and approved for seating.
8. Review expected reservations and note birthdays, special requests, mobility needs, or manager follow-up.
9. Confirm the approved opening greeting, wait-time communication process, and manager escalation method.
10. Notify the manager when the host stand and guest-arrival area are ready for opening.


1. Host Stand Opening SOP - VERIFICATION &
CUSTOMIZATION

□ Entrance and host stand are clean, organized, and guest-ready.
□ Reservation and waitlist technology is working.
□ Current floor plan and server sections are available.
□ Menus and guest materials are current and sufficient.
□ Accessibility routes and seating options are known.
□ Opening host can explain escalation and seating-decision authority.
• Opening without reviewing reservations or section changes.
• Leaving outdated signs, menus, or handwritten notes visible to guests.
• Failing to test phones, pagers, or reservation equipment.
• Seating before tables, paths, or accessibility needs are verified.
• Not informing the manager about equipment, cleanliness, or staffing problems.
• Approved greeting, wait, reservation, or seating language: ____________________________________________
• Reservation, waitlist, pager, floor-chart, or communication system: ________________________________
• Seating authority and manager escalation role: ___________________________________________________
• Wait-time, greeting, update, or table-readiness target: ___________________________________________
• Accessibility, occupancy, patio, or local guest-access requirement reviewed: ______________________
• Restaurant layout, table, entrance, or large-party adjustments: ___________________________________

REVISION NOTE: Record future changes in the restaurant master SOP index and retrain affected employees when the approved


2. Host Stand Closing SOP

SOP ID V1-002 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To close, clean, secure, document, and prepare the host stand and guest-arrival area for the next
operating period.
APPLIES TO: Closing hosts, lead hosts, and managers responsible for front-door operations.
RESPONSIBILITY: The closing host completes the process. The closing manager verifies equipment, records, lost
items, and final building condition.
WHEN USED: At the end of the final seating period or when the manager releases the host position.
• Closing checklist
• Reservation and waitlist system
• Lost-and-found container or log
• Cleaning supplies approved for the host area
• Charging cords, key storage, and manager communication log
• No guest information, reservation details, payment information, or employee notes are left unsecured.
• All equipment is charged, stored, or powered down according to restaurant policy.
• The entrance and lobby remain guest-ready until the final guest has departed.
• Unresolved reservations, complaints, lost property, or facility concerns are handed directly to the manager.
1. Confirm with the manager that seating is closed or that the host position may be released.
2. Review the remaining guest count, open tables, late reservations, waiting parties, and expected pickups.
3. Finish greeting, departure recognition, phone coverage, and guest assistance until reassigned.
4. Close the waitlist and reservation day according to the approved system process.
5. Document no-shows, late cancellations, special requests, and manager follow-up items.
6. Collect menus, wipe approved surfaces, organize supplies, and remove trash from the host area.
7. Inspect the lobby, entry path, door area, and seating equipment; clean or report anything unfinished.
8. Process found property according to the lost-and-found SOP.
9. Charge, secure, or power down phones, tablets, pagers, radios, and other equipment.
10. Give the manager a final handoff and obtain closing verification before leaving.


2. Host Stand Closing SOP - VERIFICATION &
CUSTOMIZATION

□ No waiting guests, reservations, or late arrivals are unaccounted for.
□ Guest and employee information is secured.
□ Equipment is charged, powered down, or stored correctly.
□ Host stand, lobby, and entry area are clean.
□ Lost property and unresolved issues are documented.
□ Manager receives a complete closing handoff.
• Closing the stand while guests still need assistance.
• Leaving reservation information or guest phone numbers visible.
• Failing to charge or secure host equipment.
• Discarding found property or failing to log it.
• Leaving the manager without information about no-shows, complaints, or late parties.
• Approved greeting, wait, reservation, or seating language: ____________________________________________
• Reservation, waitlist, pager, floor-chart, or communication system: ________________________________
• Seating authority and manager escalation role: ___________________________________________________
• Wait-time, greeting, update, or table-readiness target: ___________________________________________
• Accessibility, occupancy, patio, or local guest-access requirement reviewed: ______________________
• Restaurant layout, table, entrance, or large-party adjustments: ___________________________________

3. Guest Greeting and Acknowledgment SOP

SOP ID V1-003 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To make every arriving guest feel noticed, welcomed, and professionally guided from the moment they
enter.
APPLIES TO: Hosts, managers, servers near the entrance, bartenders, and any employee who sees an arriving
guest before the host does.
RESPONSIBILITY: The host leads the greeting. Every employee shares responsibility for immediate
acknowledgment when the host is occupied.
WHEN USED: Whenever a guest enters, approaches the host stand, calls for arrival assistance, or appears
uncertain about where to go.
• Approved greeting language
• Reservation and waitlist system
• Current floor plan and table-status information
• Manager communication method
• Guests are acknowledged promptly even when the employee cannot immediately complete the seating process.
• The greeting is warm, clear, respectful, and free from arguments, personal conversations, or distracted behavior.
• Employees do not make assumptions about party size, reservation status, accessibility needs, or seating preferences.
• Wait times and next steps are communicated honestly.
1. Stop nonessential conversation and make eye contact with the arriving guest.
2. Offer the approved greeting and welcome the party to the restaurant.
3. Ask how many guests are in the party and whether they have a reservation.
4. Confirm the reservation name, party size, or walk-in status without announcing private information loudly.
5. Ask whether the party has accessibility, child-seating, high-chair, booster, or other seating needs.
6. Check table availability, reservation timing, server rotation, and manager restrictions.
7. If immediate seating is available, explain the next step and escort the party.
8. If a wait is necessary, quote the approved wait time and explain how updates will be provided.
9. If delayed by another task, acknowledge the guest and state that someone will assist them shortly.
10. Thank the guest for arriving and maintain professional attention until the handoff is complete.


3. Guest Greeting and Acknowledgment SOP -
VERIFICATION & CUSTOMIZATION

□ Employee acknowledges arriving guests promptly.
□ Approved greeting is used naturally and professionally.
□ Reservation and party information is confirmed accurately.
□ Accessibility and seating needs are asked respectfully.
□ Wait or seating next steps are clear.
□ Employee knows when to involve the manager.
• Ignoring guests while finishing a personal conversation or looking at a screen.
• Pointing toward a table instead of guiding the guest.
• Using casual, dismissive, or overly familiar language.
• Announcing guest names, phone numbers, or special requests loudly.
• Giving an unrealistic wait time to avoid a difficult conversation.
• Approved greeting, wait, reservation, or seating language: ____________________________________________
• Reservation, waitlist, pager, floor-chart, or communication system: ________________________________
• Seating authority and manager escalation role: ___________________________________________________
• Wait-time, greeting, update, or table-readiness target: ___________________________________________
• Accessibility, occupancy, patio, or local guest-access requirement reviewed: ______________________
• Restaurant layout, table, entrance, or large-party adjustments: ___________________________________

4. Seating Rotation SOP

SOP ID V1-004 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To seat guests fairly and efficiently while balancing server workload, reservations, table availability, and
guest needs.
APPLIES TO: Hosts, lead hosts, shift leaders, and managers controlling the dining-room seating flow.
RESPONSIBILITY: The host follows the approved rotation. The manager authorizes major rotation changes,
section closures, large-party placement, or intentional double-seating.
WHEN USED: During all seating periods, especially rushes, shift changes, reservation blocks, and large-party
arrivals.
• Current floor plan
• Server section map
• Reservation list
• Waitlist and table-status system
• Manager seating notes
• Guests are never seated at dirty, unsafe, unstable, or unapproved tables.
• Rotation is followed when possible, but guest needs, party size, reservations, and operational capacity take priority.
• Repeated double-seating or skipping a server requires communication and manager awareness.
• Rotation changes are recorded or clearly communicated.
1. Review active server sections and confirm which servers are ready to receive tables.
2. Identify closed tables, reserved tables, large-party setups, accessibility seating, and temporary restrictions.
3. Use the approved rotation to select the next eligible section.
4. Confirm the selected table is clean, set, safe, and appropriate for the party size.
5. Consider the server's current workload, recent seating, and ability to provide timely service.
6. Seat the party and update the chart, system, or rotation tracker immediately.
7. Communicate special requests, accessibility needs, children, celebrations, or time-sensitive concerns to the server.
8. When guests request a specific table or server, adjust the rotation fairly and document the change.
9. Do not double-seat or overload a section without approval unless immediate action is required for guest safety or service
recovery.
10. Notify the manager when seating flow, kitchen capacity, or server workload requires a larger adjustment.


4. Seating Rotation SOP - VERIFICATION &
CUSTOMIZATION

□ Host understands active sections and rotation order.
□ Table status is updated immediately after seating.
□ Guest and accessibility needs are considered.
□ Server workload is monitored.
□ Rotation changes are fair and communicated.
□ Host asks the manager for help before service is damaged.
• Skipping servers without a documented reason.
• Repeatedly double-seating one section.
• Seating dirty or unapproved tables.
• Failing to update the seating chart.
• Ignoring kitchen capacity, large parties, or reservation commitments.
• Approved greeting, wait, reservation, or seating language: ____________________________________________
• Reservation, waitlist, pager, floor-chart, or communication system: ________________________________
• Seating authority and manager escalation role: ___________________________________________________
• Wait-time, greeting, update, or table-readiness target: ___________________________________________
• Accessibility, occupancy, patio, or local guest-access requirement reviewed: ______________________
• Restaurant layout, table, entrance, or large-party adjustments: ___________________________________

5. Waitlist Management SOP

SOP ID V1-005 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To organize waiting parties, provide honest time expectations, protect guest information, and seat
parties in a fair and controlled order.
APPLIES TO: Hosts, lead hosts, managers, and any employee authorized to add, update, notify, or remove parties
from the waitlist.
RESPONSIBILITY: The host maintains accurate entries and updates. The manager controls major quote changes,
seating pauses, and guest-recovery decisions.
WHEN USED: Whenever immediate seating is unavailable or when the manager activates a waitlist.
• Approved waitlist system
• Current floor plan and table status
• Guest notification device or method
• Reservation schedule
• Manager escalation process
• Wait times are estimates, not promises, and are updated when conditions change.
• Guest names and contact information are handled discreetly.
• Parties are not removed without following the approved contact and grace-period process.
• Reservations, accessibility needs, party size, and arrival order are managed according to restaurant policy.
1. Confirm the party size, preferred name, contact method, seating needs, and whether all party members must be present.
2. Check current table status, reservation commitments, server capacity, kitchen conditions, and manager quote guidance.
3. Provide the approved estimated wait time and explain that timing may change.
4. Enter the party accurately into the waitlist and repeat the information for confirmation.
5. Explain where the party may wait, how notification will occur, and the grace period after notification.
6. Update table status and wait estimates as parties are seated, tables are reset, or service conditions change.
7. Provide reasonable updates to waiting guests, especially when the estimate changes materially.
8. Notify parties using the approved method and record the notification time.
9. If the party does not respond, follow the grace-period and second-contact process before removal.
10. Escalate disputes, long delays, accessibility concerns, or service-recovery needs to the manager.


5. Waitlist Management SOP - VERIFICATION &
CUSTOMIZATION

□ Entries include correct party size and contact information.
□ Wait quotes reflect current operations.
□ Guests receive clear notification and grace-period instructions.
□ Waitlist order and exceptions follow policy.
□ Updates are communicated when conditions change.
□ Private guest information is not exposed.
• Giving an exact promise instead of an estimate.
• Adding the wrong party size or phone number.
• Removing parties too quickly.
• Failing to update guests when the wait increases.
• Allowing friends, regulars, or demanding guests to bypass policy without manager approval.
• Approved greeting, wait, reservation, or seating language: ____________________________________________
• Reservation, waitlist, pager, floor-chart, or communication system: ________________________________
• Seating authority and manager escalation role: ___________________________________________________
• Wait-time, greeting, update, or table-readiness target: ___________________________________________
• Accessibility, occupancy, patio, or local guest-access requirement reviewed: ______________________
• Restaurant layout, table, entrance, or large-party adjustments: ___________________________________

6. Reservation Handling SOP

SOP ID V1-006 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To accept, confirm, update, seat, and document reservations accurately while protecting restaurant
capacity and guest expectations.
APPLIES TO: Hosts, reservation agents, managers, and employees authorized to create or change reservations.
RESPONSIBILITY: The employee entering the reservation verifies all details. The manager approves exceptions,
large parties, deposits, special seating promises, or capacity overrides.
WHEN USED: When a reservation is requested, confirmed, modified, canceled, checked in, or marked as a no-
show.
• Reservation system or approved reservation book
• Capacity and floor-plan rules
• Large-party and deposit policies
• Special-event schedule
• Manager contact method
• No seating location, table number, exact timing, or special request is guaranteed unless authorized.
• Guest names, phone numbers, payment details, and notes are protected.
• Changes are documented immediately.
• Large parties, accessibility needs, allergies, celebrations, and time constraints are clearly noted and communicated.
1. Ask for the reservation date, preferred time, party size, guest name, and contact information.
2. Check availability, pacing rules, special events, section restrictions, and large-party limits.
3. Explain available times and any deposit, cancellation, arrival, seating, or time-limit policy.
4. Enter the reservation and repeat the date, time, party size, name, and contact information for confirmation.
5. Record accessibility needs, allergies, high chairs, celebrations, mobility concerns, or other relevant notes.
6. Do not promise a specific table or area without manager authorization.
7. For changes, verify the guest identity and update every affected field.
8. Send or provide the approved confirmation and arrival instructions.
9. At check-in, confirm the name and party status, then update the reservation system.
10. Document cancellations, no-shows, late arrivals, and manager-approved exceptions.


6. Reservation Handling SOP - VERIFICATION &
CUSTOMIZATION

□ Reservation details are complete and repeated back.
□ Capacity and pacing rules are followed.
□ Special needs and important notes are visible to the correct team.
□ Unauthorized promises are not made.
□ Changes and cancellations are recorded immediately.
□ Guest information is protected.
• Entering the wrong date, time, or party size.
• Promising a specific table without approval.
• Failing to document accessibility or allergy information.
• Overbooking without manager direction.
• Discussing guest contact or payment information where others can hear.
• Approved greeting, wait, reservation, or seating language: ____________________________________________
• Reservation, waitlist, pager, floor-chart, or communication system: ________________________________
• Seating authority and manager escalation role: ___________________________________________________
• Wait-time, greeting, update, or table-readiness target: ___________________________________________
• Accessibility, occupancy, patio, or local guest-access requirement reviewed: ______________________
• Restaurant layout, table, entrance, or large-party adjustments: ___________________________________

7. Phone Answering SOP

SOP ID V1-007 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To answer restaurant calls promptly, professionally, accurately, and consistently while protecting guest
information and avoiding operational disruption.
APPLIES TO: Hosts, managers, bartenders, to-go employees, and any employee assigned to phone coverage.
RESPONSIBILITY: The assigned employee answers and routes calls. The manager handles complaints, sensitive
employee matters, media calls, emergencies, and policy exceptions.
WHEN USED: During all posted phone-answering hours and whenever the restaurant line rings while staff are
present.
• Working phone and charger
• Approved greeting
• Current hours, directions, menu, reservation, and to-go information
• Message form or system
• Manager contact method
• The caller receives a clear greeting, the restaurant name, and the employee's name when required.
• Calls are not placed on hold without permission and an estimated return.
• Private employee schedules, phone numbers, guest records, and internal information are not disclosed.
• Emergency, threat, injury, complaint, and media calls are escalated immediately.
1. Answer within the restaurant's approved ring standard whenever possible.
2. Use the approved greeting and speak clearly at a professional volume.
3. Ask how you may help and listen without interrupting.
4. Confirm the purpose of the call before transferring or placing the caller on hold.
5. Ask permission before placing the caller on hold and return within the approved time.
6. Provide only current, approved information about hours, location, menu, reservations, events, and policies.
7. For messages, record the caller name, number, reason, time, urgency, and intended recipient.
8. Do not provide employee schedules, personal contact information, guest information, or internal security details.
9. Escalate complaints, legal threats, media requests, emergencies, suspicious calls, and sensitive employee matters to the
manager.
10. Close the call by confirming the next step and thanking the caller.


7. Phone Answering SOP - VERIFICATION &
CUSTOMIZATION

□ Employee uses the approved greeting.
□ Calls are answered or returned within the standard.
□ Hold and transfer procedures are professional.
□ Messages are complete and legible.
□ Confidential information is protected.
□ Employee recognizes calls requiring manager escalation.
• Answering with only "hello" or using an unprofessional tone.
• Leaving callers on hold without returning.
• Giving outdated hours or policy information.
• Sharing employee schedules or personal information.
• Arguing with a caller instead of escalating.

8. Guest Seating and Escort SOP

SOP ID V1-008 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To escort guests safely and professionally from the host stand to an approved, ready table while
communicating essential information to the service team.
APPLIES TO: Hosts, lead hosts, managers, and any employee assigned to seat guests.
RESPONSIBILITY: The seating employee verifies the table and escorts the party. The manager resolves unsafe
seating, accessibility, section, or guest-request conflicts.
WHEN USED: Whenever a party is seated in the dining room, bar, patio, private room, or other approved area.
• Menus and approved guest materials
• Current table and section status
• Accessibility route knowledge
• High chairs, boosters, or other approved seating equipment
• The table and travel path are clean, clear, safe, and appropriate for the party.
• The employee walks at a pace the entire party can follow and does not leave guests behind.
• Accessibility, child-seating, and mobility needs are handled respectfully.
• The server or responsible team member is informed of the seating and relevant guest needs.
1. Confirm the selected table is clean, fully set, stable, and approved for the party size.
2. Gather the correct number and type of menus or guest materials.
3. Invite the party to follow and identify the area where they are being seated.
4. Choose the safest, clearest path and walk at a comfortable pace.
5. Monitor that children, older guests, and guests with mobility needs remain with the party.
6. Assist with chairs, high chairs, boosters, or accessibility arrangements according to policy.
7. Present the table and allow guests to settle before distributing menus.
8. Confirm that the seating arrangement is acceptable without pressuring the guest.
9. Inform the server or section team that the party is seated and communicate important notes.
10. Update the seating system or chart immediately.


8. Guest Seating and Escort SOP - VERIFICATION &
CUSTOMIZATION

□ Table and path are checked before escorting.
□ Guest pace and accessibility needs are respected.
□ Menus and seating equipment are correct.
□ Host remains with the party until seated.
□ Server is informed promptly.
□ Seating chart is updated immediately.
• Pointing to a table instead of escorting.
• Walking too quickly or leaving part of the party behind.
• Seating at an unclean or unstable table.
• Blocking walkways with high chairs or mobility devices.
• Failing to tell the server that the table has been seated.

9. Accessibility Seating and Accommodation SOP

SOP ID V1-009 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To respond respectfully and effectively to seating and access needs without assumptions,
embarrassment, delay, or unsafe arrangements.
APPLIES TO: Hosts, servers, managers, and employees assisting guests with mobility, sensory, communication, or
other access needs.
RESPONSIBILITY: The host offers available options. The manager handles complex requests, conflicts,
unavailable accommodations, or safety concerns.
WHEN USED: Whenever a guest requests an accommodation or an access need becomes apparent during arrival,
seating, or service.
• Knowledge of accessible entrances, routes, tables, and restrooms
• Approved accommodation policy
• Portable seating equipment if applicable
• Manager contact method
• Employees ask what assistance is needed rather than guessing.
• Accessible routes, exits, aisles, and service-animal access are not blocked.
• Mobility devices and personal equipment are not moved without permission.
• Requests are handled privately and escalated respectfully when the employee cannot resolve them.
1. Acknowledge the request and ask what seating or assistance would work best.
2. Review available accessible tables, routes, entrances, and restroom access.
3. Offer practical options without describing the request loudly or drawing unnecessary attention.
4. Do not touch, lean on, move, or separate mobility devices or personal assistance equipment without permission.
5. Maintain clear aisles and emergency exits when adjusting tables or chairs.
6. If a requested table is unavailable, explain available alternatives and involve the manager.
7. Communicate only necessary information to the server or manager.
8. Allow additional time for seating and do not rush the guest or party.
9. Follow restaurant policy and applicable accessibility requirements regarding service animals and reasonable accommodations.
10. Document unresolved issues or operational barriers for manager follow-up.


9. Accessibility Seating and Accommodation SOP -
VERIFICATION & CUSTOMIZATION

□ Employee asks rather than assumes.
□ Accessible routes and seating are known.
□ Guest equipment is respected.
□ Information is communicated discreetly.
□ Manager is involved when options are limited.
□ Barriers or unresolved concerns are documented.
• Assuming a guest needs help without asking.
• Speaking to a companion instead of the guest.
• Moving a wheelchair, walker, or other device without permission.
• Blocking aisles or exits to create a quick seating arrangement.
• Refusing a request without manager review.

10. Large Party Seating SOP

SOP ID V1-010 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To plan and seat large parties safely, fairly, and efficiently without disrupting reservations, server
workload, kitchen capacity, or nearby guests.
APPLIES TO: Hosts, lead hosts, managers, servers, service assistants, and employees responsible for large-party
setup.
RESPONSIBILITY: The manager approves the table plan, server assignment, timing, and exceptions. The host
controls arrival, check-in, and seating communication.
WHEN USED: For parties meeting the restaurant's large-party threshold or requiring multiple tables, special setup,
deposits, contracts, or dedicated staffing.
• Reservation details and contact information
• Approved floor plan and table-combination rules
• Large-party policy
• Server assignment and kitchen communication plan
• High chairs, boosters, accessibility equipment, or event materials
• Tables are combined only in approved configurations that maintain aisles, exits, and service access.
• Server and kitchen capacity are confirmed before seating.
• Deposits, minimums, time limits, gratuity policies, and payment arrangements are explained according to law and company
policy.
• The party is not partially seated unless the restaurant policy allows it or the manager approves.
1. Review the reservation, party size, arrival time, seating needs, payment plan, and special requests.
2. Confirm the approved table layout, aisle clearance, accessibility, and service path.
3. Verify server assignment, support staff, menu restrictions, and kitchen readiness.
4. Prepare menus, place settings, child seating, celebration items, and approved signs before arrival.
5. At check-in, confirm the party name, final size, arrival status, and whether seating requirements are met.
6. Communicate delays or incomplete-party rules clearly and respectfully.
7. Seat the party using the approved route and help organize seating without creating congestion.
8. Introduce the primary server or manager when required.
9. Update the reservation and seating systems and notify the kitchen or bar of the arrival.
10. Document material changes, no-shows, policy exceptions, or guest concerns.

CUSTOMIZATION

□ Table layout is approved and safe.
□ Server and kitchen capacity are confirmed.
□ Party policies are explained accurately.
□ Required setup is complete before seating.
□ Arrival and communication are organized.
□ Exceptions are documented and manager-approved.
• Combining tables in a way that blocks aisles or exits.
• Seating without confirming server or kitchen readiness.
• Making up deposit, gratuity, or time-limit rules.
• Failing to communicate a party-size change.
• Allowing a large party to overwhelm one server without support.

11. Table Assignment and Section Balancing SOP

SOP ID V1-011 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To assign available tables and sections in a way that supports fair workload, timely service,
reservations, staffing, and guest needs.
APPLIES TO: Hosts, lead hosts, managers, and supervisors creating or adjusting the floor plan.
RESPONSIBILITY: The manager approves the floor plan and major changes. The host maintains the working
assignment and communicates real-time conditions.
WHEN USED: Before opening, at shift change, when staffing changes, or whenever sections must be adjusted
during service.
• Floor plan
• Employee schedule
• Reservation forecast
• Table capacity and accessibility information
• Manager labor and service notes
• Assignments reflect employee readiness and workload, not favoritism.
• Closed sections and restricted tables are clearly marked.
• High-volume, large-party, bar, patio, and accessibility areas have adequate coverage.
• Changes are communicated to affected employees before the next seating whenever possible.
1. Review scheduled servers, arrival times, training status, and planned cuts.
2. Review reservations, expected sales, large parties, patio conditions, and special events.
3. Assign sections with comparable opportunity and manageable travel distance when possible.
4. Identify tables requiring experienced coverage, accessibility access, or special handling.
5. Mark closed, held, or unavailable tables clearly.
6. Review assignments with servers and confirm understanding.
7. Adjust assignments when call-offs, cuts, guest flow, or service problems change capacity.
8. Communicate every change to hosts, servers, service assistants, kitchen leadership, and managers as needed.
9. Update the floor plan immediately.
10. Record major recurring imbalance or layout problems for future planning.


11. Table Assignment and Section Balancing SOP -
VERIFICATION & CUSTOMIZATION

□ Assignments match staffing and demand.
□ Sections are balanced and clearly marked.
□ Special areas have appropriate coverage.
□ Employees receive changes promptly.
□ Floor plan remains current.
□ Manager reviews recurring section or workload problems.
• Creating sections based on favoritism.
• Giving a trainee an unmanageable section.
• Changing sections without telling the host or server.
• Leaving inaccessible or unavailable tables open in the system.
• Failing to rebalance after cuts or call-offs.

12. Wait-Time Quoting and Update SOP

SOP ID V1-012 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To provide realistic, consistent wait estimates and timely updates based on current restaurant
conditions.
APPLIES TO: Hosts, lead hosts, managers, and employees authorized to quote or revise wait times.
RESPONSIBILITY: The host uses approved guidance and current conditions. The manager authorizes major quote
changes, seating pauses, and recovery decisions.
WHEN USED: Whenever immediate seating is unavailable or a quoted wait changes materially.
• Waitlist and table-status system
• Reservation schedule
• Server and kitchen capacity information
• Manager quote guidance
• Quoted times are honest estimates, not guarantees.
• Quotes account for reservations, party size, table availability, reset time, server capacity, and kitchen conditions.
• Material delays are communicated before the guest must ask whenever possible.
• Employees do not shorten quotes merely to prevent a guest from leaving.
1. Review current waiting parties, open tables, reservation commitments, and average table-turn conditions.
2. Check with the manager when kitchen delays, staffing problems, large parties, or table restrictions affect capacity.
3. Provide the approved estimated range and explain that it may change.
4. Enter the quoted time and quote timestamp in the waitlist system.
5. Monitor table status and actual seating pace.
6. When conditions change materially, revise the internal estimate and notify affected guests.
7. Explain the cause of delay briefly without blaming employees or discussing private operational details.
8. Offer approved options such as bar seating, patio seating, takeout, or a later reservation when available.
9. Escalate severe delays, disputes, accessibility concerns, or recovery needs to the manager.
10. Record the actual seating time for future quote accuracy review.


12. Wait-Time Quoting and Update SOP -
VERIFICATION & CUSTOMIZATION

□ Quotes reflect real-time conditions.
□ Estimate and quote time are recorded.
□ Guests receive updates when delays change materially.
□ Employees avoid exact promises.
□ Approved alternatives are offered appropriately.
□ Manager is involved when quotes become unreliable.
• Quoting from habit without checking current conditions.
• Reducing the quote to keep a party from leaving.
• Failing to account for reservations or large parties.
• Waiting for the guest to complain before giving an update.
• Blaming the kitchen or a specific employee for the delay.

13. Special Seating Request SOP

SOP ID V1-013 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To handle requests for booths, windows, patios, quiet areas, specific servers, or other seating
preferences fairly and without unauthorized promises.
APPLIES TO: Hosts, reservation agents, managers, and employees assisting with seating requests.
RESPONSIBILITY: The host records and attempts reasonable requests. The manager decides conflicts,
unavailable requests, and exceptions that affect operations.
WHEN USED: When a guest makes a seating request before arrival, at check-in, or during seating.
• Floor plan and table-status information
• Reservation notes
• Accessibility information
• Manager escalation method
• Preferences are treated as requests unless specifically guaranteed by an authorized manager.
• Accessibility needs are distinguished from general preferences and handled under the accessibility SOP.
• Employees do not promise a shorter wait or displace another reservation without approval.
• Specific-server requests are handled fairly and with server availability in mind.
1. Listen to the request and clarify what matters most to the guest.
2. Determine whether the request is a general preference, accessibility need, or special-event arrangement.
3. Check table status, reservations, section capacity, weather, and manager restrictions.
4. Explain whether the request is available immediately, requires a longer wait, or cannot be provided.
5. Offer practical alternatives when the preferred option is unavailable.
6. Record the request in the reservation or waitlist system.
7. Do not move, remove, or hold tables in a way that creates a safety or service problem.
8. For specific-server requests, confirm the server is working and able to accept the table.
9. Escalate disputes, accessibility concerns, or promised arrangements to the manager.
10. Update the seating plan and communicate approved changes.


13. Special Seating Request SOP - VERIFICATION &
CUSTOMIZATION

□ Employee distinguishes needs from preferences.
□ Requests are recorded accurately.
□ Unauthorized guarantees are avoided.
□ Alternatives are offered.
□ Server and section capacity are considered.
□ Manager resolves conflicts and prior promises.
• Guaranteeing a booth, patio table, or specific server without authority.
• Treating an accessibility need as a simple preference.
• Holding a table so long that service flow is damaged.
• Making another guest wait unfairly without manager approval.
• Arguing instead of offering alternatives.

14. Child Seating Equipment and Safety SOP

SOP ID V1-014 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To provide clean, stable, correctly placed high chairs, boosters, and other approved child-seating
equipment while protecting aisles and guest safety.
APPLIES TO: Hosts, service assistants, servers, and managers handling child-seating equipment.
RESPONSIBILITY: The employee providing the equipment inspects and places it. The manager removes damaged
equipment from use and resolves unsafe placement.
WHEN USED: Whenever a party requests a high chair, booster, sling, or other approved child-seating aid.
• Approved high chairs and boosters
• Cleaning and sanitizing supplies
• Inspection tag or out-of-service label
• Safe storage area
• Damaged, unstable, dirty, or unapproved equipment is never used.
• Equipment does not block aisles, exits, server paths, or accessibility routes.
• Employees do not place carriers on chairs or equipment unless the restaurant has an approved device and policy.
• The adult guest remains responsible for securing and supervising the child.
1. Ask which approved seating equipment the party needs.
2. Select equipment that is clean, dry, stable, and appropriate for the requested use.
3. Inspect straps, buckles, feet, joints, trays, and surfaces for damage or missing parts.
4. Clean and sanitize according to the approved chemical and contact-time procedure.
5. Place the equipment at an approved table location that does not block traffic or exits.
6. Explain any restaurant policy regarding use, placement, or securing the child.
7. Allow the adult guest to position or secure the child unless assistance is requested and policy allows.
8. Remove damaged equipment from service immediately and notify the manager.
9. After use, clean, sanitize, dry, and return the equipment to approved storage.
10. Document recurring damage, shortages, or unsafe placement concerns.


14. Child Seating Equipment and Safety SOP -
VERIFICATION & CUSTOMIZATION

□ Equipment is inspected before use.
□ Cleaning and sanitizing standards are followed.
□ Aisles and exits remain clear.
□ Damaged equipment is removed and reported.
□ Adult supervision responsibility is respected.
□ Equipment is stored clean and dry.
• Using a high chair with broken straps or unstable feet.
• Placing equipment in a service aisle or exit path.
• Using a chair or overturned booster as a carrier stand.
• Returning dirty equipment to storage.
• Failing to report missing or damaged parts.

15. Lobby and Entry Area Readiness SOP

SOP ID V1-015 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To keep the restaurant entrance, lobby, waiting area, and guest-arrival path clean, safe, welcoming,
and operational throughout service.
APPLIES TO: Hosts, service assistants, managers, and employees assigned to lobby checks.
RESPONSIBILITY: The host monitors the area. The manager coordinates repairs, weather response, crowd
control, and hazards that cannot be corrected immediately.
WHEN USED: Before opening, during scheduled checks, after spills or weather changes, and whenever guest
traffic increases.
• Lobby inspection checklist
• Approved cleaning supplies
• Wet-floor signs
• Entry mats and umbrella storage if used
• Manager communication method
• Entrances, exits, mats, floors, and waiting paths remain clear and safe.
• Wet floors, broken glass, damaged furniture, and crowding are addressed immediately.
• Signage, menus, and promotional materials are current and professional.
• Guest privacy and accessibility are protected in the waiting area.
1. Inspect exterior and interior entry paths, door glass, handles, mats, floors, lights, and signage.
2. Remove clutter, trash, outdated signs, personal items, and unapproved materials.
3. Confirm chairs, benches, rails, and tables are clean, stable, and positioned safely.
4. Check that accessible routes, doors, and seating remain open and usable.
5. Monitor weather conditions and adjust mats, signs, and floor checks as needed.
6. Clean spills immediately or guard the area until the correct employee arrives.
7. Manage waiting-party placement so doors, exits, host operations, and service paths remain clear.
8. Keep music, lighting, temperature, and displayed materials within restaurant standards.
9. Report facility, lighting, door, pest, odor, or security concerns to the manager.
10. Record unresolved hazards and confirm follow-up.

□ Mats and floors are safe.
□ Accessible routes are open.
□ Waiting guests do not block doors or exits.
□ Current signs and materials are displayed.
□ Hazards are corrected, guarded, and documented.
• Allowing wet mats or floors to remain unmarked.
• Blocking accessibility routes with chairs or waiting parties.
• Displaying handwritten or outdated signs without approval.
• Ignoring damaged furniture or door problems.
• Leaving the entrance unattended during a rush.

16. Table Status Communication SOP

SOP ID V1-016 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To keep hosts, servers, service assistants, managers, and reservation systems aligned on whether
each table is occupied, clearing, resetting, held, reserved, unavailable, or ready.
APPLIES TO: Hosts, servers, service assistants, bussers, managers, and employees updating table status.
RESPONSIBILITY: The employee changing a table's condition updates or communicates the status immediately.
The host maintains the authoritative seating view.
WHEN USED: Whenever a party is seated, a table is vacated, cleaning begins, reset is completed, or a table
becomes unavailable.
• Table-status system or chart
• Floor plan
• Radio, verbal, or approved communication method
• Out-of-service marker if applicable
• A table is marked ready only after it is fully cleaned, reset, stable, and approved.
• Status changes are immediate and use standard terms.
• Reserved, held, unsafe, or out-of-service tables are clearly identified.
• Conflicting information is resolved before seating.
1. Use the restaurant's approved status terms and colors.
2. Mark a table occupied immediately after seating.
3. When guests leave, communicate that the table is open for clearing, not ready for seating.
4. Update the status when clearing and reset begin.
5. Inspect the completed reset for cleanliness, stability, correct settings, and surrounding floor condition.
6. Mark the table ready only after the final check.
7. Identify held, reserved, accessibility, large-party, or out-of-service tables clearly.
8. Communicate unusual delays or damage to the host and manager.
9. Resolve system and visual-status conflicts before directing a party to the table.
10. Review table status during rushes and shift handoffs.


16. Table Status Communication SOP -
VERIFICATION & CUSTOMIZATION

□ Standard status terms are used.
□ Table-status changes are communicated and entered immediately.
□ Ready means fully inspected and set.
□ Held and unavailable tables are clear.
□ Conflicts are resolved before seating.
□ Host, floor team, and manager share the same information.
• Marking a table ready before cleaning is complete.
• Using vague terms such as "almost ready" without an update.
• Failing to mark a table out of service.
• Updating the system but not communicating a major delay.
• Seating based on assumption rather than confirmed status.

17. Guest Departure Recognition SOP

SOP ID V1-017 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To close the guest experience with sincere appreciation, assistance, and awareness of unresolved

APPLIES TO: Hosts, servers, managers, bartenders, and any employee positioned near a departing guest.

• Manager escalation method
• Departing guests are acknowledged without blocking their path or forcing conversation.
• Unresolved dissatisfaction, forgotten property, mobility needs, and safety concerns are addressed immediately.
• Employees remain professional even when the guest has complained or service was difficult.

2. Offer the approved farewell and thank the guest for visiting.
3. Open or hold the door when safe and appropriate.
4. Offer reasonable assistance with directions, weather, accessibility, or found property.
5. Watch for visible dissatisfaction or an attempt to speak with a manager.
6. If a guest mentions a concern, listen briefly and involve the manager rather than dismissing it.
7. Do not ask about gratuity or defend service performance at the exit.
8. Confirm the party has personal belongings and child-seating items.
9. Keep the doorway and exit path clear.
10. Communicate serious concerns or repeat feedback to the manager.


17. Guest Departure Recognition SOP -

□ Exit path remains clear.
□ Employees recognize unresolved concerns.
□ Assistance is offered appropriately.
□ Difficult guests are still treated respectfully.
□ Feedback requiring follow-up reaches the manager.
• Ignoring departing guests while using a phone or talking to coworkers.
• Blocking the exit to continue a conversation.
• Asking whether the guest tipped or enjoyed a specific employee.
• Arguing about a complaint at the door.
• Failing to tell the manager about serious negative feedback.

18. Lost and Found Intake SOP

SOP ID V1-018 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To receive, document, secure, match, and return found property consistently while protecting guest
privacy and reducing loss or theft risk.
APPLIES TO: Hosts, servers, managers, and employees who find or receive guest property.
RESPONSIBILITY: The finding employee turns the item over immediately. The manager or designated custodian
controls storage, identification, release, and disposal.
WHEN USED: Whenever property is found, reported missing, claimed, transferred, or disposed of under policy.
• Lost-and-found log
• Secure storage area
• Item tag or evidence bag
• Manager contact information
• Employees do not search through, use, photograph, or keep found property.
• Cash, identification, payment cards, medications, weapons, and high-value items are given directly to the manager.
• Claimants must describe the item or provide reasonable proof before release.
• Guest contact information and item details are kept private.
1. Do not leave the found item unattended or continue carrying it through the restaurant.
2. Notify the manager or designated custodian immediately.
3. Record the date, time, location found, employee name, item description, and condition.
4. Place the item in an approved tag, bag, or container without unnecessary handling.
5. Transfer cash, identification, cards, medication, keys, electronics, weapons, or high-value items directly to the manager.
6. Store the item in the approved secure location.
7. When a guest calls, request a description rather than revealing all item details.
8. For return, verify the description, record claimant information, and obtain the required signature.
9. Do not mail, ship, or release items to another person without manager approval.
10. Follow the restaurant retention and disposal policy for unclaimed items.


18. Lost and Found Intake SOP - VERIFICATION &
CUSTOMIZATION

□ Item is logged promptly.
□ High-risk items go directly to the manager.
□ Storage is secure.
□ Claimant verifies identifying details.
□ Release is documented.
□ Retention and disposal follow policy.
• Looking through a wallet, bag, or phone unnecessarily.
• Leaving found property at the host stand.
• Describing the item in detail to an unverified caller.
• Returning an item without documentation.
• Discarding property before the retention period.

19. Weather-Related Entry and Patio Guest
Management SOP

SOP ID V1-019 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To protect guests and operations when rain, wind, heat, cold, lightning, ice, smoke, or other weather
affects the entrance, waiting area, or patio.
APPLIES TO: Hosts, patio servers, managers, service assistants, and employees controlling guest movement
during weather changes.
RESPONSIBILITY: The manager decides patio closure, seating pauses, relocation, and emergency action. The
host communicates approved options and maintains safe entry flow.
WHEN USED: Before opening and whenever weather conditions change or affect guest safety, comfort, access, or
table availability.
• Weather information source
• Wet-floor signs and mats
• Patio closure or relocation plan
• Reservation and waitlist systems
• Manager communication method
• Lightning, high wind, ice, flooding, extreme temperature, poor air quality, or other hazards are escalated immediately.
• Wet entrances and floors are guarded and corrected.
• Guests are not promised patio seating when weather may require closure.
• Relocations are coordinated so aisles, occupancy limits, and server capacity remain controlled.
1. Review weather conditions and manager patio guidance before opening.
2. Inspect entry mats, drainage, doors, umbrellas, patio furniture, heaters, fans, and lighting.
3. Place approved mats and signs where moisture or ice may create a hazard.
4. Advise arriving guests when patio availability is uncertain or weather-dependent.
5. Monitor conditions and report rapid changes immediately.
6. When the manager closes the patio, stop new seating and update reservations, waitlist quotes, and floor plans.
7. Coordinate relocation of seated guests according to table availability and manager priority.
8. Keep entry doors, exits, accessibility routes, and emergency paths clear during crowding.
9. Document damaged equipment, guest incidents, or reservations materially affected by weather.
10. Reopen the patio only after manager inspection and approval.


19. Weather-Related Entry and Patio Guest
Management SOP - VERIFICATION &
CUSTOMIZATION

□ Weather is reviewed before and during service.
□ Wet or icy entry hazards are controlled.
□ Guests receive accurate patio information.
□ Closure and relocation decisions are manager-led.
□ Systems and floor plans are updated.
□ Patio reopens only after inspection.
• Continuing patio seating after unsafe conditions begin.
• Failing to update quoted wait times after losing patio capacity.
• Moving guests without coordinating table and server capacity.
• Leaving wet floors unmarked.
• Allowing furniture or umbrellas to remain unsecured in wind.

20. Host Shift Handoff SOP

SOP ID V1-020 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To transfer accurate guest, reservation, waitlist, table, equipment, and manager information between
host employees without service interruption.
APPLIES TO: Outgoing and incoming hosts, lead hosts, and managers overseeing the transition.
RESPONSIBILITY: The outgoing host gives a complete handoff. The incoming host confirms understanding and
immediately assumes control of the position.
WHEN USED: At scheduled host changes, breaks, cuts, meal periods, or emergency position changes.
• Reservation and waitlist system
• Current floor plan and table status
• Manager communication log
• Equipment and key inventory
• The host stand is never left without clear coverage.
• Waiting parties, late reservations, table holds, accessibility needs, and guest concerns are reviewed individually.
• The incoming host verifies the current state instead of relying only on verbal memory.
• Private information is discussed discreetly.
1. Notify the manager or lead host that a handoff is beginning.
2. Review current waiting parties, quoted times, contact status, and special needs.
3. Review upcoming, late, canceled, and no-show reservations.
4. Review table status, server rotation, cuts, section changes, held tables, and large-party plans.
5. Identify complaints, VIPs, accessibility requests, weather issues, and manager follow-up.
6. Count and transfer pagers, radios, keys, tablets, phones, and other controlled items.
7. Show the incoming host unresolved equipment, cleanliness, or facility concerns.
8. The incoming host checks the systems and repeats critical information back.
9. Update the log with the handoff time and both employee names.
10. Do not leave until the incoming host accepts control or the manager approves release.


20. Host Shift Handoff SOP - VERIFICATION &
CUSTOMIZATION

□ Coverage remains continuous.
□ Waitlist and reservations are reviewed.
□ Floor plan and rotation are current.
□ Special guest and manager notes are transferred.
□ Equipment and keys are accounted for.
□ Handoff is documented.
• Leaving for a break before the incoming host is ready.
• Giving only a general summary such as "everything is fine."
• Failing to mention a late large party or long-wait complaint.
• Discussing private guest notes loudly.
• Not counting controlled equipment.

21. Server Opening Side Work SOP

SOP ID V1-021 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To prepare each server station, assigned section, and shared service area before guests are seated.
APPLIES TO: Opening servers, assigned side-work employees, trainers, shift leads, and managers.
RESPONSIBILITY: Each server completes assigned work. The manager or shift lead verifies readiness before
release to service.
WHEN USED: Before opening, before a server takes a section, and after any major shift reset.
• Current opening side-work chart or checklist
• Approved cleaning and sanitizing supplies
• Napkins, silverware, glassware, condiments, and service supplies
• Menus, check presenters, pens, receipt paper, and to-go supplies
• POS access, section map, reservation notes, and manager communication
• Opening work is complete before the first assigned table is seated.
• Guest-contact items are clean, undamaged, and handled according to sanitation policy.
• Stations are stocked to approved pars without blocking aisles or emergency routes.
• Shortages, damage, or incomplete work are reported before service begins.
1. Review the side-work assignment, section map, and expected business level.
2. Wash hands and gather only approved cleaning and stocking supplies.
3. Inspect the assigned station; remove trash, old receipts, clutter, and personal items.
4. Clean and sanitize approved station surfaces, shelves, trays, and touchpoints.
5. Stock service supplies to the restaurant par and rotate usable stock when required.
6. Inspect tables, chairs, booths, floors, menus, and table settings in the assigned section.
7. Test the POS login, printer, handheld, and payment equipment assigned to the station.
8. Review reservations, large parties, unavailable items, and special service notes.
9. Report missing supplies, equipment problems, safety hazards, or incomplete shared work.
10. Complete the checklist and obtain manager or shift-lead verification before seating.


21. Server Opening Side Work SOP - VERIFICATION &

□ Required service supplies are stocked to the approved par.
□ POS, printer, and payment equipment are working.
□ Tables, chairs, menus, and settings meet the restaurant standard.
□ Shortages and maintenance concerns are documented or escalated.
□ Server is released only after opening work is verified.
• Waiting until guests arrive to begin stocking.
• Using unapproved chemicals or dirty towels on guest-contact areas.
• Overstocking stations until aisles, shelves, or exits are blocked.
• Ignoring damaged tableware, unstable furniture, or equipment problems.
• Signing off work that another employee has not actually completed.

22. Server Pre-Shift Readiness SOP

SOP ID V1-022 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To confirm that every server begins the shift professionally prepared, informed, equipped, and ready to
serve guests.
APPLIES TO: All servers, server trainers, shift leads, and managers.
RESPONSIBILITY: The server completes personal and operational readiness. The manager verifies exceptions
and communicates shift priorities.
WHEN USED: Before clock-in readiness review, pre-shift meeting, or assignment to a section.
• Uniform and appearance standards
• Pens, order pad, wine key, handheld, or other approved tools
• Current menu, beverage, feature, and unavailable-item information
• Allergen communication process and approved ingredient resources
• Section assignment, reservation notes, bank, and POS credentials
• Employees report ready to work at the scheduled start time, not begin preparing after it.
• Uniform, hygiene, and required tools meet the approved standard.
• Servers do not guess about ingredients, allergens, prices, or promotions.
• Banks, devices, and credentials are controlled and verified before use.
1. Arrive with enough time to store personal items and meet the scheduled ready time.
2. Inspect uniform, grooming, footwear, name tag, and required protective items.
3. Gather and test required service tools; replace missing or unsafe items.
4. Log into the POS or handheld and confirm the correct employee profile and printer.
5. Count and acknowledge any assigned bank according to cash-control policy.
6. Review menu changes, features, shortages, pricing, and beverage information.
7. Review allergy alerts, ingredient questions, and the approved escalation process.
8. Confirm section, table numbers, seat-number method, reservations, and large parties.
9. Attend pre-shift, ask questions, and repeat back critical shift information when requested.
10. Notify the manager immediately if any readiness requirement cannot be completed.


22. Server Pre-Shift Readiness SOP - VERIFICATION

□ Menu features, shortages, and service priorities are understood.
□ Server can explain the allergy and guest-concern escalation process.
□ Assigned bank and controlled equipment are verified.
□ Section and table-number responsibilities are understood.
• Clocking in and then leaving the floor to get ready.
• Borrowing tools throughout the shift instead of arriving equipped.
• Using another employee's POS credentials or bank.
• Guessing about menu items or relying on outdated feature information.
• Skipping pre-shift because the server has worked the menu before.

23. Server Table Greeting SOP

SOP ID V1-023 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To acknowledge and welcome every newly seated table promptly while establishing the service plan for
the meal.
APPLIES TO: Servers, bartenders serving tables, trainers, shift leads, and managers providing coverage.
RESPONSIBILITY: The assigned server owns the greeting. A covering employee or manager greets when the
server is delayed.
WHEN USED: Immediately after seating and again when a delayed server assumes the table.
• Current menu and beverage knowledge
• Feature, shortage, and promotion information
• Order pad, handheld, or approved service device
• Allergen and ingredient escalation resources
• Restaurant greeting and timing standards
• No table remains unacknowledged while employees debate ownership.
• The greeting is warm, professional, and adjusted to the guest's pace.
• Servers never guess about allergens, ingredients, alcohol rules, or pricing.
• Any service delay is acknowledged honestly and ownership is established.
1. Approach the table within the restaurant greeting-time standard.
2. Make eye contact, smile naturally, and acknowledge every guest when practical.
3. Welcome the table and introduce yourself according to the restaurant standard.
4. Confirm that guests have the correct menus and needed seating accommodations.
5. Offer water or beverages and identify time-sensitive features or unavailable items.
6. Ask whether the table has immediate questions or allergy concerns.
7. Listen without interrupting and use the approved resource for uncertain answers.
8. Take the beverage order or give the table a clear time for your return.
9. Enter urgent beverage or allergy information without unnecessary delay.
10. Return as promised and continue service or transfer ownership clearly.


23. Server Table Greeting SOP - VERIFICATION &
CUSTOMIZATION

□ Table is greeted within the approved time target.
□ Every guest is acknowledged respectfully.
□ Beverages and important menu information are offered.
□ Questions and allergy concerns are handled without guessing.
□ Delayed service is acknowledged and a return time is given.
□ Server follows through after the greeting.
• Greeting only one person and ignoring the rest of the table.
• Using a rushed script without listening to the guest.
• Leading with a long sales speech before offering beverages.
• Promising an immediate return and then disappearing.
• Assuming another employee greeted the table without confirming.

24. Menu Presentation and Guidance SOP

SOP ID V1-024 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To present accurate menu information and help guests make informed choices without pressure or
unsupported claims.
APPLIES TO: Servers, bartenders, trainers, and managers who discuss food or beverage choices.
RESPONSIBILITY: The employee provides approved information. Managers or designated leaders resolve
uncertain ingredient, price, or policy questions.
WHEN USED: During greeting, ordering, feature presentation, and any guest menu question.
• Current food, beverage, dessert, and children's menus
• Approved feature and unavailable-item information
• Ingredient, preparation, and allergen reference
• Pairing, portion, substitution, and upcharge guidance
• Manager escalation method
• Only current menus and approved descriptions are used.
• Descriptions are accurate and do not promise outcomes the kitchen cannot deliver.
• Ingredient and allergy questions are verified through approved sources.
• Upcharges, limitations, and unavailable items are explained before ordering.
1. Provide the correct menu for each guest and note accessibility needs.
2. Briefly identify features, limited items, or unavailable items at the proper time.
3. Ask open questions about preferences, timing, portion, or beverage interests.
4. Recommend items using approved descriptions and actual menu knowledge.
5. Explain preparation style, included sides, options, and known upcharges clearly.
6. Use the approved ingredient source for dietary or allergen questions.
7. Do not label an item safe, allergen-free, or guaranteed unless policy specifically permits it.
8. Offer alternatives when an item cannot meet the guest's request.
9. Confirm the guest understands significant substitutions, add-ons, or price differences.
10. Escalate unresolved questions before accepting the final order.


24. Menu Presentation and Guidance SOP -
VERIFICATION & CUSTOMIZATION

□ Current menus and feature information are being used.
□ Server descriptions match approved recipes and preparation methods.
□ Upcharges and substitution limits are explained before entry.
□ Allergy and ingredient questions are verified, not guessed.
□ Recommendations match the guest's stated needs and pace.
□ Unresolved questions are escalated before the order is placed.
• Using exaggerated descriptions that the product cannot support.
• Failing to disclose an upcharge or required substitution.
• Calling a menu item allergen-free based on memory.
• Continuing to sell an item already marked unavailable.
• Pressuring guests instead of helping them decide.

25. Beverage Order Taking SOP

SOP ID V1-025 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To capture complete and accurate beverage orders while following service, age-verification, and guest-
preference standards.
APPLIES TO: Servers, bartenders, cocktail servers, trainers, and managers.
RESPONSIBILITY: The order-taking employee records the request, confirms details, and follows alcohol-service
policy when applicable.
WHEN USED: At the initial greeting and whenever additional beverages are requested.
• Current beverage menu and pricing
• Feature, brand, size, garnish, and refill information
• Order pad, seat-number method, or handheld
• Age-verification and responsible-service procedures
• Allergy and ingredient reference for beverage components
• Every beverage is assigned to the correct seat or guest.
• Alcohol orders follow identification and responsible-service requirements.
• Brands, sizes, modifiers, temperatures, and garnishes are clarified.
• Uncertain ingredients or allergy concerns are verified before entry.
1. Ask for beverage orders in an organized seat or position sequence.
2. Clarify brand, size, preparation, temperature, ice, mixer, and garnish as needed.
3. Identify whether refills are complimentary, charged, or restricted by policy.
4. For alcohol, complete required age verification before submitting the order.
5. Record each beverage directly rather than relying on memory.
6. Repeat unusual, high-cost, allergy-related, or heavily modified requests.
7. Confirm which guest receives each beverage.
8. Enter the order promptly using the correct menu key and modifiers.
9. Communicate unavailable items or delays and offer approved alternatives.
10. Monitor preparation and follow up if the beverage exceeds the service-time target.

□ Orders are entered without avoidable delay.
• Writing a general list with no seat positions.
• Assuming the size, brand, mixer, or garnish.
• Submitting alcohol before completing required identification checks.
• Failing to explain charged refills or premium substitutions.
• Waiting to enter beverages until after taking the food order.

26. Food Order Taking SOP

SOP ID V1-026 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To obtain complete food orders that accurately reflect guest selections, required choices, modifications,
and timing needs.
APPLIES TO: Servers, bartenders taking food orders, trainers, and managers.
RESPONSIBILITY: The order-taking employee asks required questions, records the order by seat, and resolves
uncertainty before entry.
WHEN USED: Whenever a guest places, changes, or adds to a food order.
• Current menu, feature, and unavailable-item information
• Seat-number or guest-position method
• Order pad, handheld, or approved device
• Required-choice, modifier, substitution, and upcharge guide
• Ingredient, allergen, and manager escalation resources
• Orders are recorded by seat and in a consistent service sequence.
• Required cooking temperatures, sides, dressings, and modifiers are obtained.
• Allergy requests follow the separate allergy communication procedure.
• Servers do not promise substitutions or preparation changes without approval.
1. Ask whether the table is ready and identify any shared or coursed items.
2. Take orders in the approved seat-number or position sequence.
3. Listen fully before suggesting choices or entering modifiers.
4. Ask every required question for temperature, side, dressing, preparation, and add-ons.
5. State any known upcharge, unavailable choice, or substitution limit.
6. Record modifications using approved terms rather than informal shorthand.
7. For allergy concerns, pause and complete the approved allergy communication process.
8. Repeat back complex, modified, high-risk, or unclear orders.
9. Confirm course timing, shared plates, children's items, and special delivery requests.
10. Thank the guests and enter the order promptly without relying on memory.

□ Upcharges and unavailable selections are communicated.
□ Allergy requests are routed through the approved process.
□ Complex orders are confirmed before the server leaves.
• Taking the entire table from memory.
• Skipping required questions and letting the kitchen guess.
• Using vague notes such as "special" without details.
• Promising a substitution before checking availability or policy.
• Combining allergy notes with ordinary preference modifiers.

SOP ID V1-027 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To verify the guest's order before submission and prevent avoidable preparation, billing, and delivery

APPLIES TO: Servers, bartenders taking food orders, trainers, and managers.

• Current menu and modifier options
• Allergy alert and special-request process

• Every item, seat, modifier, course, and quantity is reviewed.
• Allergy notes remain clear and separate from preference notes.
• Guests are informed about material price or preparation changes.
• Unclear requests are resolved before the order is sent.
1. Review the order in the same seat sequence used while taking it.
2. Confirm item name, quantity, cooking temperature, side, dressing, and add-ons.
3. Check that removals and substitutions are specific and possible.
4. Verify shared items, children's meals, courses, and requested firing instructions.
5. Read back complex or modified orders in clear guest-friendly language.
6. Confirm allergy communication and manager involvement where required.
7. Check that each item is assigned to the correct seat or check.
8. Resolve conflicting notes, duplicate items, or missing required choices.
9. Confirm the guest understands significant upcharges or unavailable requests.
10. Submit only after the order matches the guest's final instructions.

□ Order is submitted only after discrepancies are resolved.
• Reading back only the entrée name and missing modifiers.
• Treating a complex order as obvious because it is familiar.
• Leaving duplicate items after a guest changes their mind.

28. POS Order Entry SOP

SOP ID V1-028 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To enter guest orders accurately, securely, and quickly so production, billing, and service timing remain
controlled.

• Authorized employee POS credentials
• Correct table, check, and seat-number information
• Current menu keys, modifiers, and pricing
• Allergy alert and course-timing functions
• Manager approval process for restricted actions
• Employees use only their own credentials and assigned permissions.
• The correct table, guest count, seat, and revenue center are selected.
• All modifiers and allergy alerts are entered using approved keys.
• The final order screen is reviewed before sending.

3. Enter beverages and food by seat in the sequence ordered.
4. Use approved modifier keys for temperatures, sides, additions, removals, and substitutions.
5. Apply the designated allergy alert and follow required verbal communication.
6. Assign courses, hold instructions, or fire times only when authorized.
7. Review quantities, prices, seats, modifiers, and special instructions.
8. Send the order to the correct production stations without unnecessary delay.
9. Confirm that tickets printed or displayed at the intended stations.
10. Report missing keys, routing failures, price errors, or duplicate sends to the manager.

□ Production tickets are received at the proper stations.
□ System or pricing problems are escalated and documented.
• Entering orders under another employee's number.
• Selecting the wrong table or leaving every item on seat one.
• Typing free-text notes when an approved modifier key exists.
• Sending an allergy note without verbal confirmation.
• Assuming the ticket routed correctly without checking a system error.

29. Course Timing and Firing SOP

SOP ID V1-029 VERSION 1.0

PURPOSE: To coordinate order release so each course arrives at the intended pace, temperature, and sequence.
APPLIES TO: Servers, bartenders serving meals, expos, kitchen leaders, and managers.
RESPONSIBILITY: The server communicates the guest pace and enters approved timing instructions. Expo and
kitchen control production release.
WHEN USED: For multi-course meals, held entrées, large parties, shared items, and any timing adjustment.
• POS course, hold, and fire functions
• Table and seat-number information
• Kitchen and expo communication method
• Restaurant course-time standards
• Manager approval process for unusual holds or refires
• Timing instructions are clear, realistic, and visible to production.
• Food is not held in unsafe or quality-damaging conditions to fix poor pacing.
• Servers monitor the table and communicate changes before the next course is fired.
• Only authorized employees alter sent-course timing.
1. Identify the table's preferred pace and any fixed timing needs.
2. Enter appetizers, entrées, desserts, and shared items in the correct courses.
3. Use approved hold or fire functions instead of vague free-text notes.
4. Inform expo or the kitchen leader of unusual pacing, large-party, or allergy needs.
5. Monitor when the current course is delivered and how quickly the table progresses.
6. Clear or prepare the table before requesting the next course.
7. Fire the next course at the approved point based on kitchen lead time.
8. Communicate delays, guest pauses, or changed plans immediately.
9. Do not leave completed food waiting while attempting to repair timing silently.
10. Confirm delivery, check quality, and document refires or major timing failures.


29. Course Timing and Firing SOP - VERIFICATION &
CUSTOMIZATION

□ Courses are entered and released in the intended sequence.
□ Kitchen and expo understand unusual timing instructions.
□ Table is prepared before the next course arrives.
□ Server monitors guest pace rather than using a fixed assumption.
□ Delays or timing changes are communicated promptly.
□ Refires and significant failures are escalated.
• Using "hold" with no clear release plan.
• Firing entrées before appetizers are delivered or cleared.
• Trying to slow service by leaving completed food in the window.
• Failing to tell the kitchen when guests leave the table temporarily.
• Promising an exact time without checking production conditions.
